#4992. Situational Awareness Dumps Bitcoin Miners After Citadel Sale

Situational Awareness, the artificial intelligence hedge fund run by former OpenAI researcher Leopold Aschenbrenner, cut its bitcoin miner holdings sharply in the third quarter, leaving about $80.8 million across three stocks and exiting three others, a Monday filing shows.

The San Francisco firm still held 3,019,029 CleanSpark shares, worth $38.9 million at Sept. 30, 1,934,618 Bitdeer shares, worth $20.3 million, and 527,864 IREN shares, worth $21.6 million. Those are the only miner lines left, a sliver of the $1.83 billion in six miner stocks the fund listed at June 30.

The deepest cut was IREN. The fund held 9,474,099 IREN shares, worth $433.3 million, at midyear. The Sept. 30 stake is about 94% smaller by share count. CleanSpark was trimmed about 75%, from 12,276,139 shares worth $178.6 million. Bitdeer was reduced about 44%, from 3,439,450 shares worth $54.6 million. Bitdeer’s reported value fell further than its share count, to $20.3 million, as the stock also declined.

Three names disappeared from the filing entirely. The fund had held 26,008,473 Core Scientific shares, worth $665.6 million, 17,100,000 Riot Platforms shares, worth $468.2 million, and 9,133,726 Hive Digital shares, worth $33.2 million, at June 30. None of those lines appear in the new report. The filing does not say whether those sales were part of a late-July portfolio sale, later trades, or both.

Aschenbrenner published a long 2024 essay, “Situational Awareness: The Decade Ahead,” arguing that artificial general intelligence was approaching faster than most investors appreciated, and that computing power and electricity would be the tight constraints. He founded the fund that September around that view, looking at technology and energy companies that AI would help or hurt.

The miner stakes fit the same idea. A March profile described the fund as betting that electricity and computing capacity may be the most valuable assets in the AI build-out, pointing to miners that were turning high-density facilities and power contracts toward AI workloads. IREN has signed a multiyear cloud contract with Microsoft. CleanSpark has struck a $6.6 billion data-center lease. Listed miners have also been operating under tight economics this year, with bitcoin often trading below estimated production costs.

The cuts landed after a brutal July. The fund sold the bulk of the fund’s public stock portfolio to Ken Griffin’s Citadel late that month after heavy losses on AI-related holdings, Reuters reported, citing people familiar with the matter. Those sources said the fund was forced to unwind most of its public book. In a letter to investors, Aschenbrenner said the portfolio was down an estimated 67% in July, that the fund had come “closer to permanent capital impairment than is acceptable,” and that it had sold a portion of its public holdings in a block trade to remove all leverage and close its shorts. The unaudited letter still put year-to-date performance at plus 80%. Aschenbrenner said the fund was not shutting down and had preserved its private investments.

The new 13F shows how much of the public book was left by Sept. 30. Reported long holdings fell from $20.24 billion, across 26 lines, at June 30, to $1.58 billion, across 23 lines, three months later. That decline should not be read as the fund losing 92% of its capital. A 13F reports only long positions in U.S.-listed stocks and certain options. It leaves out cash, shorts and private investments, and option lines are valued at the underlying shares, not the premiums paid. Reuters’ sources said that after the Citadel deal the fund would hold a book of roughly $10 billion, including stocks and private investments such as a stake in Anthropic.

What remains is concentrated. The largest common-stock line is now SharonAI, 8,070,950 shares worth $440.7 million. Aschenbrenner also listed call options on Bloom Energy, SanDisk, Nebius and a memory ETF. The three leftover miner stakes are a stub of the midyear book. Whether those positions were trimmed further after Sept. 30 will only be clear when the fourth-quarter 13F arrives in mid-February.

#4991. BYD is launching this 5-seat luxury electric SUV next week with over 1,000 km of range

BYD’s luxury brand, Denza, calls it “the king of large 5-seater pure electric vehicles.” The new Denza N8 electric SUV will launch next week, offering an impressive CLTC driving range of over 1,000 km (621 miles), massive digital screens, and plenty of premium extras.

After opening pre-orders for the new 5-seat luxury electric SUV last week, Li Hui, Denza’s general manager, said the results so far “have been excellent.”

According to Li, the Denza N8 is “definitely the most anticipated luxury SUV of the year.” Using BYD’s second-generation Blade Batteries, the Denza N8 EV has a pure electric CLTC range of up to 1,003 km (623 miles) on a single charge.

That’s just as much as the N8 plug-in hybrid (PHEV) it’s replacing, which offered a combined CLTC range of 1,003 km (623 miles) on a full tank of gas and a full charge.

While Li confirmed last week that the new EV variant would go on sale in November, he didn’t give a specific date.

Now, it’s official. Denza announced on its Weibo page on Friday that the launch event for the Denza N8 EV will be held on November 12, 2026.

BYD is still offering a limited-time promo that includes a 5,000 RMB credit for those who place a reservation with a 2,000 RMB ($200) deposit.

The Denza N8 measures 5,150 mm long, 1,999 mm wide, and 1,820 mm high, with a 3,075 mm wheelbase. That’s slightly longer than the Kia EV9 and Hyundai IONIQ 9.

It’s available with a single rear-wheel-drive (RWD) or a tri-motor all-wheel-drive (AWD) powertrain. The RWD version uses a single motor producing either 320 kW (429 hp) or 370 kW (496 hp).

The tri-motor Denza N8 uses BYD’s e3 platform. The setup includes three electric motors: a 270 kW (362 hp) front motor and two 310 kW (416 hp) rear motors, for a combined output of 890 kW (1,193 hp).

Regulatory filings earlier this year revealed it will use either a 130.15 kWh or 105.79 kWh BYD Blade 2.0 battery, offering a CLTC range of up to 1,003 km (623 miles) and 800 km (497 miles), respectively.

The Denza N8 also features BYD’s latest Flash Charging technology, letting it recharge from 10% to 80% in just five minutes. It takes just nine minutes to go from 10% to 97%.

Inside, the five-seat electric SUV features a massive 30″ infotainment screen that spans nearly the entire passenger-side dashboard, plus a new 43″ (1.1 meter) panoramic head-up display (PHUD) at the bottom of the windshield.

Like most of its new luxury vehicles, the Denza N8 is loaded with BYD’s latest software and technology.

Added premium features include zero-gravity seating in all rows, a three-spoke steering wheel, wireless phone chargers, and ambient lighting.

The electric SUV is equipped with a roof-mounted LiDAR to support BYD’s God’s Eye 5.0 intelligent driving assistance system, plus its DiSus-A intelligent air suspension system.

BYD has yet to announce prices for the Denza N8 EV, but it will likely be slightly less than the N8L, an extended version that starts at 299,800 RMB ($45,000).

#4988. Morning Minute: The CFTC Officially Greenlights Perps in the US, Starting with Backpack

Morning Minute: The CFTC Officially Greenlights Perps in the US, Starting with Backpack

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

🏛️ Backpack and tZERO Win the First Onchain Derivatives Approvals

For years, perps were the product American traders couldn’t legally touch. On Monday, that officially changed.

Chairman Michael Selig approved applications from Backpack and tZERO to run regulated derivatives exchanges and clearinghouses that execute and settle on blockchain rails. Selig called it “an important milestone in the evolution of American financial markets.”

Backpack got both pieces at once, a Designated Contract Market designation to run the exchange and a Derivatives Clearing Organization registration to clear its own trades. It plans to offer Americans round-the-clock leveraged crypto perps on a single global order book, accepting both fiat and crypto as collateral with cross-margin across positions. tZERO received its exchange approval a week earlier, after already holding clearing registration, and is building it into a derivatives vertical inside its infrastructure business.

Backpack US president Mark Wetjen, himself a former CFTC commissioner, framed it as “moving price discovery from offshore unregulated venues to the United States.” CEO Armani Ferrante said the firm enters markets “fully regulated from day one.” Backpack already runs licensed platforms in Europe, Asia and the UAE.

The CFTC has certainly been busy this month. They cleared single-stock perpetual futures on November 3. It issued guidance on November 5 telling federally regulated exchanges they generally cannot restrict access by state. Now it has approved blockchain-native exchanges and clearinghouses.

Selig is building an onshore home for every product the offshore venues were built to serve, and he is doing it through staff action rather than legislation, with one sitting commissioner and nomination hearings for two more this week.

Hyperliquid is the obvious candidate and also the one that has publicly said it doesn’t want this. Jeff Yan spent his Bloomberg interview this month arguing Hyperliquid is infrastructure rather than an exchange, and that regulators should oversee the firms deploying markets on top rather than the chain underneath. His US route is HIP-3 partners taking on the compliance burden, with ICE investing in Trade.xyz and Kraken’s parent reportedly in talks. Backpack getting a DCM and a DCO makes that argument harder, because it demonstrates a crypto-native venue can simply register.

Then there’s Lighter. It has run Robinhood Wallet’s perps since July on a dedicated instance with a 50/50 revenue split, which is the partner model rather than the registration model. The registered model opens up with a CFTC license, and many think Lighter could very well be next.

One thing’s certain—the CFTC is serious about bringing perps onshore. From Selig himself, “I look forward to building on that milestone.” And we will find out soon enough who’s next…

#4986. Fomo pays creators $30 million, sharing ‘lion’s share’ of revenue, Se Yong Park says

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Co-founder Se Yong Park says Fomo has paid roughly $10 million a month to creators who hold assets and explain their trades.

Crypto trading app Fomo has paid out $30 million in creator rewards to date to users who share their trading ideas.

Speaking with The Block on Tuesday at the Solana Breakpoint conference in London, Fomo co-founder Se Yong Park said the payouts have averaged roughly $10 million a month since the program launched.

"It's a lion’s share of our revenue, and we think it’s important to continue to go back and invest in the creators who are making the platform great," he said.

The program rewards users for sharing their investment hypotheses while holding the tokens they're discussing.

"You have to be holding it to earn the Creator Awards," Park said. "You write a thesis, which is very, very important as a word, because you want to actually articulate why are you buying it? What is the reasoning?"

In a separate announcement on Tuesday, the company said it had approximately 3.6 million registered users and 1.5 million monthly active users. Cumulative trading volume has grown to more than $32 billion since the app launched in May 2025.

Memecoins are still the platform's bread and butter. Park put their share of trading activity at between 60%-70%, though he didn't specify the exact metric. Park also pointed to growing interest in tokenized stocks and other assets on the platform.

"We’re starting to see more and more diversification pretty much every week," he said.

Fomo was founded by three dYdX veterans and raised $75 million in a June Series B led by Index Ventures at a $550 million valuation. The platform has raised roughly $94 million to date.

#4984. Too funded to fail: Crypto needs a forest fire

For new growth, crypto may need to shed tired norms like over-raising and the hoarding of investment resources

#4983. Standard Life Chair Wants Tokenized Gilts to Open UK Government Bonds to Retail Savers

Standard Life Chair Wants Tokenized Gilts to Open UK Government Bonds to Retail Savers

Tokenization could help ordinary British savers buy government bonds more easily, and should not become a tool only for big financial institutions, according to Sir Nicholas Lyons, chairman of Standard Life.

"Why should it be so unnecessarily complicated for retail investors to buy gilts that fund the nation's needs?" Lyons said at Solana's Breakpoint conference in London.

Lyons, a former Lord Mayor of the City of London whose company has about £480 billion ($633 billion) in assets under administration, said Britain should use tokenization to bring more households into investing. About £850 billion ($1.1 trillion) of household wealth remains in cash, often losing value to inflation, he said.

Tokenization could cut administrative costs, make transfers simpler and let savers invest in small amounts in products that would otherwise be uneconomic, Lyons said. More retail ownership of gilts would make the UK less reliant on foreign buyers and could help erode the risk premium the country has paid on its debt since the September 2022 mini-budget, he added.

The remarks come as the UK prepares to issue its first digitally native government bond, known as DIGIT. Earlier this month, the Treasury confirmed roles for six banks supporting the pilot, with issuance expected in the first quarter of 2027, Lyons said.

"Government bonds sit at the heart of our financial system," he said, noting that they underpin liquidity, provide collateral and set reference rates for other assets. Showing that tokenized gilts can be issued, settled and used as collateral efficiently would lay "an important foundation for much broader digital capital markets," he said.

Lyons credited the Financial Conduct Authority, the Bank of England and the Treasury for progress so far, including the Digital Securities Sandbox and work to let tokenized securities be used as collateral.

The Bank of England has also turned its attention to stablecoins: an executive has said it is treating them as a new form of money, it eased its planned stablecoin rules in June, and it has since been handed a legal duty to foster stablecoin innovation. Lyons cited a July report by Chris Woolard, the government's wholesale digital markets champion, which estimated that faster adoption of digital markets could add as much as £33 billion ($43.5 billion) a year to UK economic output by 2035.

But the competition is moving fast, he warned, pointing to the SEC's innovation exemption for tokenized stock trading in the U.S., and initiatives in Europe, Singapore, Hong Kong, Switzerland and the Middle East.

Lyons set out four priorities: making the digital gilt pilot a success, accelerating tokenized collateral and repo markets, promoting interoperability to avoid "a collection of competing digital platforms that recreate the fragmentation of the existing system," and ensuring retail investors share in the benefits.

He also acknowledged the risks, citing concerns raised by the Financial Times' Gillian Tett that AI-accelerated cyber threats and faster-moving money could fuel a run on deposits. "Tokenization does not automatically make an investment safer, more liquid, or more stable, or suitable for retail investors," he said. "Fractional ownership of a risky asset remains ownership of a risky asset."

"We're not pursuing tokenization for the sake of technological leadership," Lyons said. "We're pursuing better capital markets, more productive investment, greater economic prosperity, and wider participation in the creation of wealth and jobs."

#4980. EVgo opens its first Tesla Superchargers, with hundreds more planned

EVgo has opened its first Supercharger station, and it’s in a Chicago grocery store parking lot. The site has 12 fast-charging stalls with both NACS and CCS plugs, and it’s the first of hundreds of Tesla Supercharger stalls EVgo plans to add across the US.

The 325 kW “EVgo Superchargers” are at Riverside Square & River’s Edge, at 3145 S Ashland Avenue. EVgo’s August announcement cited up to 500 kW for the Supercharger rollout.

The Chicago location features Magic Dock technology, which means both NACS and CCS vehicles can charge without an adapter. Drivers of compatible non-Tesla EVs can start a session through the Tesla app. If you drive a Tesla, the charging station shows up in the car’s navigation and Trip Planner. Other EVgo fast chargers with NACS plugs have been showing up in Tesla navigation since early September if you turn on third-party charging.

“By serving both Tesla and non-Tesla NACS drivers, we’re doubling EVgo’s addressable market and continuing to meet EV drivers where they are and where they want to charge,” said Badar Khan, EVgo CEO.

EVgo owns the Superchargers and self-brands them, and Tesla runs and maintains them. The first sites will be at Regency Centers shopping plazas with grocery stores.

Smart move on EVGo's part. More reliable charger at a lower cost than they can build themselves, and Tesla takes on all the maintenance and network operations for a slice of the sales.

EVgo is also adding NACS plugs to its regular fast-charging network, a rollout it started in 2025. The company aims to have 500 of those stalls across the US by the end of 2026, with Supercharger stalls counted separately. Over the next two years, it plans to put at least one NACS plug at every EVgo site built in 2023 or later.

#4979. Coinbase expands onchain crypto loans to New York via Morpho

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Coinbase Borrow is now available in New York, letting residents borrow against their crypto through Morpho on Base.

Crypto exchange operator Coinbase has expanded its onchain crypto loan product to New York residents, completing its rollout across all 50 U.S. states.

The launch follows approval from the New York State Department of Financial Services, a Coinbase spokesperson told The Block.

Asked when Coinbase applied and received approval, Jacob Frantz, head of Coinbase's yield and investment products, said the company had been working with New York regulators since the product launched last year.

"We have been working closely with regulators in New York since launching Coinbase Borrow last year, so that New Yorkers can also access cash without having to sell the crypto assets they believe in," Frantz told The Block. He did not provide application or approval dates or details on the scope of the approval.

With Coinbase Borrow, eligible New York residents can borrow USDC against supported crypto held on Coinbase. The borrowing limits are up to $5 million against bitcoin, $1 million against ether and $100,000 against Solana, subject to eligibility and other applicable limits, the Coinbase spokesperson said.

Customers access Coinbase Borrow through Coinbase, with Morpho providing the onchain lending infrastructure on Base.

The product launched in January 2025 and has since originated more than $3.4 billion in loans. More than $1.7 billion remains outstanding, backed by over $3.2 billion in collateral, Morpho said.

Coinbase has added more collateral options since the launch, most recently tokenized gold via Paxos' Pax Gold (PAXG). It has also introduced fixed-rate loans through Morpho Midnight, allowing borrowers to agree on a rate and maturity before taking out a loan.

Those features are not all available in New York. At launch, customers in the state can borrow against BTC, ETH and SOL, the Coinbase spokesperson said.

Fixed-rate loans and other collateral options, including tokenized gold PAXG, Zcash's ZEC, Hyperliquid's HYPE, XRP and staked assets, are not yet available there, the spokesperson added.

Coinbase also offers a separate USDC lending product through Morpho, where users can earn returns by allocating their USDC across onchain lending markets. That product holds more than $500 million in deposits, according to Morpho, but is also unavailable in New York.

Asked about plans to bring those products to the state, the spokesperson said: "We're excited to continue partnering with regulators to bring better financial services to New Yorkers."

#4976. Jito’s Lucas Bruder says crypto builders need to ‘slow down’ and fix security weaknesses

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Jito is expanding its security team and using AI for defense as Bruder calls the next two years critical for crypto builders.

Jito Labs co-founder and CEO Lucas Bruder says crypto developers need to pump the brakes on building to focus on security as attackers use AI tools to find weaknesses in their defenses.

Speaking to The Block on Tuesday at the Solana Breakpoint conference in London, Bruder said the next two years are going to be pivotal for teams to fix unresolved issues in their software, which he referred to as "technical debt."

"I’m a fan of moving fast, but now I think the next two years are gonna be super critical to slow down a little bit, fix your tech debt, go back and solve it," he said.

Bruder noted that Jito has been working on bolstering its security operation for the past year and a half. The company hired a security lead from Galaxy, added another security employee six months ago, and is now looking to hire two more.

"It's very intense out there," he said. "We've seen that the people that are doing this are very patient and very motivated. And so you just have to be very careful at a lot of layers of security."

For crypto companies in particular, this could mean improving how they manage multi-sig wallets, deploy software, and tighten hiring procedures.

"It's really a process that you need in your company," Bruder said. "So it’s not an afterthought, it’s like you have to bake it into the way that you work."

AI is also now being put to use on both sides of the security war. Asked about using AI to review code for vulnerabilities, Bruder said Jito has been doing similar work internally.

"On one hand, like they are using LLMs to attack you and on the other side, you can use the same LLM to help defend yourself," he said.

"You really just have to be proactive," Bruder added. "Don’t wait, just get ahead of it."

#4974. Former Robinhood Engineer Pleads Guilty to Crypto Insider Trading Scheme

Former Robinhood Engineer Pleads Guilty to Crypto Insider Trading Scheme

Former Robinhood software engineer Huaisong “Jerry” Xiang pleaded guilty Monday to wire fraud for using confidential plans for upcoming cryptocurrency listings to trade on Hyperliquid. The 30-year-old agreed to forfeit $125,523 in profits from the scheme, according to the Justice Department.

Xiang entered his plea before U.S. District Judge Colleen McMahon on November 16, two months after prosecutors accused him and another Robinhood engineer of trading ahead of the brokerage's token announcements. He pleaded guilty to one count of wire fraud; the DOJ announcement did not report a guilty plea to the commodities-fraud charge initially brought against him.

As a software engineer working on new listings for Robinhood Crypto, Xiang had access to a private Slack channel reserved for employees classified as “Coin Aware Individuals.” It contained information about which tokens were coming to the platform and when, according to the FBI complaint filed in September.

Robinhood's internal policy prohibited that group from trading around upcoming listings on any platform, not just Robinhood. The restriction began before a listing was announced and continued for 24 hours after it became public. A November 2024 email warned Xiang to “refrain from abusing access to or knowledge of this information,” the complaint says.

Investigators described one trade involving POPCAT, a Solana-based meme coin. After receiving internal messages about a planned March 13, 2025 listing, Xiang moved approximately 18 Ethereum, then worth about $34,000, into a Hyperliquid wallet and opened a bullish position tied to POPCAT, according to the complaint.

He closed that position at a profit after the token became available on Robinhood but before the company published its listing announcement, the FBI alleged. The complaint identified at least 10 other occasions through February 2026 when the same wallet traded ahead of announcements, including positions involving MEW, MOODENG, ONDO and RENDER.

Hyperliquid's perpetual futures let traders speculate on a cryptocurrency's price without owning the token itself. Unlike conventional futures, the contracts have no expiration date, and a “long” position gains value if the underlying token's price rises.

New listings can increase a token's exposure to buyers and put upward pressure on its price. Prosecutors said Xiang used his advance knowledge to position himself for those moves, sometimes closing his trades before the public knew Robinhood had added the asset.

“No matter the trading platform, corporate insiders will be held accountable when they trade based on misappropriated information,” U.S. Attorney Jamie McDonald said in Monday's announcement.

Robinhood said in September that it had investigated the matter, reported it to authorities and maintained a zero-tolerance policy on insider trading.

Xiang faces a statutory maximum of 20 years in prison, although the judge will decide his sentence. He is scheduled to appear before McMahon for sentencing on February 25, 2027.

U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs took in a net $7.9 million on Monday, ending a seven-session outflow streak. Wells Fargo more than doubled its bitcoin ETF stake to $2.35 billion, and Goldman Sachs did the same, lifting its holdings to $1.28 billion.

Anthropic is expected to price its IPO on Wednesday, with shares set to start trading Thursday. A move back above $65,000 would reverse Tuesday’s decline, while a break of the weekend low near $62,000 would put last week’s flush back in view.

#4969. Hyundai’s $359 IONIQ 5 lease looks like a deal, but don’t overlook its bigger sibling

The Hyundai IONIQ 5 is the most popular EV in America outside of Tesla, but its larger three-row sibling looks like the better deal right now.

Through September, the Hyundai IONIQ 5 was the third top-selling EV in the US, behind the Tesla Model Y and Model 3.

Hyundai’s electric SUV is outselling rivals like the Chevy Equinox EV, Ford Mustang Mach-E, and Toyota bZ.

Why? For one, it’s one of the most affordable EVs in the US, starting at about $35,000, and Hyundai has been offering some of the most attractive lease deals.

After the $7,500 federal EV tax credit expired at the end of September last year, Hyundai responded by reducing prices on the IONIQ 5 by up to nearly $10,000 on some trims.

The 2027 Hyundai IONIQ 5 starts at $35,250. That’s for the standard-range IONIQ 5 SE with an EPA-estimated range of up to 245 miles. Upgrading to the extended-range SE RWD model adds $2,500, starting at $37,750, and offers up to 318 miles of range.

Hyundai’s three-row electric SUV, the IONIQ 9, starts at $58,955, or nearly $24,000 more than the base IONIQ 5 SE.

On Hyundai’s website, the 2027 IONIQ 5 SE Standard Range is available to lease from $359 a month. The offer is for a 24-month lease with $4,999 due at signing for an effective cost of $567 per month.

The extended-range Hyundai IONIQ 5 SE RWD can be leased from $369 a month. The offer is also a 24-month lease with $4,999 due at signing, for an effective cost of $577 per month.

Hyundai’s larger IONIQ 9, meanwhile, is available to lease from $419 a month. That deal also includes $4,999 due at signing, but is for a 36-month lease, so the effective cost is $557 per month.

Using Hyundai’s payment calculator, it would cost about $412 a month to lease the 2027 Hyundai IONIQ 5 SE for 36 months with $4,999 due at signing. With an effective cost of $550 per month, it’s only $7 less than the three-row IONIQ 9.

The Hyundai IONIQ 9 offers more range than its smaller sibling, with an EPA-estimated range of up to 335 miles for the base SE trim.

The IONIQ 5 is a midsize electric SUV, about the same size as a Tesla Model Y, with two rows and enough room for five passengers. Hyundai’s larger IONIQ 9 offers three rows of seating and six- or seven-seat configurations.

With an added row of seating, the IONIQ 9 offers up to 163.54 cubic feet of interior passenger volume, compared with the IONIQ 5, which offers up to 106.5 cubic feet.

While the IONIQ 9 may be the better deal right now, the IONIQ 5 is still a good fit for those looking for a smaller, more efficient SUV that doesn’t necessarily need the added space.

In comparison, the standard Tesla Model Y RWD is listed for lease starting at $499 a month with an EPA range of up to 321 miles. That’s for a 36-month lease with $3,000 down.

Using Tesla’s payment calculator, if you put the same $4,999 down for a 24-month lease like the IONIQ 5, the Model Y RWD’s monthly rate is $549.

Which one would you choose? The IONIQ 5, IONIQ 9, or Tesla Model Y? Let us know why in the comments.

Both the IONIQ 5 and IONIQ 9 are built at Hyundai’s Metaplant America outside of Savannah, Georgia.

I really haven’t seen much value in leasing. I’d rather buy the car outright using cash, with the goal of keeping the car for 10+ years.

My family recently bought a Hyundai IONIQ 5, and it is an outstanding car. Loaded with great features — both on the EV side and then for safety and comfort.

When we toured the facility earlier this year, Hyundai told us that its electric SUVs are attracting new buyers to the brand at a higher rate than its gas or hybrid vehicles.

The IONIQ 5 had a 69.8% conquest rate last year, while the IONIQ 9 had 64.3%. Hyundai’s Elantra N ranked third (61.9%), followed by the Palisade (61.2%) and Kona Electric (60.9%).

#4967. Trump’s CFTC picks face Senate scrutiny as prediction market turf war heats up

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

If confirmed, the nominees are likely to face continued scrutiny from lawmakers over the CFTC’s authority to regulate prediction markets.

President Donald Trump’s nominees to the Commodity Futures Trading Commission revealed how they view the hotly contested issue of how to regulate sports betting as the agency continues its pursuit to oversee those markets.

During a Senate Agriculture Committee nomination hearing for Trump's Democratic Party pick, Matthew Rowland and his Republican pick, Nathan Anonick, lawmakers honed in on prediction markets and how the pair would address the agency's regulation of sports betting as states and the federal government butt heads over who should take charge.

"I actually do think the law is quite clear that jurisdiction over sports gambling rests in the hands of states and also with tribes — and yet prediction markets continue to operate, I believe in violation of these state and tribal laws," said Sen. Tina Smith, D-Minn.

If confirmed, Anonick and Rowland are likely to face continued scrutiny from lawmakers over the CFTC’s authority to regulate prediction markets, as a growing legal battle over the platforms’ oversight plays out in the courts. Under Chair Michael Selig, the CFTC has asserted that it has "exclusive jurisdiction" over event contracts. Through proposed rulemaking, the CFTC has said that prediction market contracts qualify as swaps because they are based on future events, putting them under federal commodities oversight rather than state gambling laws.

States, meanwhile, argue that prediction market platforms are operating illegal gambling businesses while sidestepping state regulations and tax obligations. Tribal organizations have also raised concerns that the platforms could circumvent existing tribal-state gaming agreements.

Nearly a dozen states and the CFTC have since sued each other, courts have taken opposing sides, and the issue could go all the way to the Supreme Court for a final decision.

During Tuesday's hearing, Rowland pointed to Regulation 40.11, which was adopted by the CFTC in 2011 and prohibits registered exchanges from listing or accepting for clearing certain event contracts involving gaming, terrorism, war and assassination.

"It's hard for me to comment on the active litigation that the CFTC is engaged in, but I'm certainly very sympathetic to the concerns that the states and tribes have raised and would want to work with them further if I were confirmed on that," Rowland added.

However, the term gaming has been the sticking point in whether it is against the public interest.  The CFTC has discretion to decide if a contract is against that public interest, Anonick said, adding that the debate is a "very charged emotional issue," and that he planned to listen to stakeholders.

"I would just argue that, friendly pushback,  that I don't think that this is an emotional issue," Smith said. "I think this is a legal issue."

If confirmed, Anonick and Rowland would join CFTC Chairman Michael Selig, the agency’s sole Republican commissioner. The CFTC and its sister agency, the Securities and Exchange Commission, have faced scrutiny over their failure to fill all five commissioner seats with bipartisan representation over the past year

Next, the Senate Agriculture Committee would have to vote on the nominees before that heads to a full Senate vote. Timing is tight ahead of a new Congress in January, and nominations would generally have to be resubmitted.

#4965. Apollo’s newest electric scooter is also its lowest-cost yet

Apollo has spent the last several years carving out a spot for itself toward the more premium end of the electric scooter market. Now the company is trying something different: going after the mass market with its least expensive model yet.

The new Apollo Dash carries a $699 MSRP, but is launching at $599 as part of the company’s Black Friday sale running through November 30. More importantly, Apollo says the Dash isn’t intended to simply be a stripped-down budget scooter, but rather a way to bring much of its existing technology down to a lower price point.

The scooter uses a 48V electrical system powering a 600W nominal, 1,200W peak-rated Bosch motor, with a top speed of 20 mph (32 km/h). Its 480Wh battery is rated for up to 25 miles (40 km) of range in Eco mode.

Apollo also claims the 48V architecture avoids limiting motor power to anemic levels as the battery drains, meaning riders shouldn’t experience the same increasingly sluggish performance toward the bottom of the battery that can occur on some lower-cost scooters.

At 43 lb (19.5 kg), the Dash is reasonably portable, though getting the weight and price down comes with an important compromise: there’s no suspension. Apollo also caps rider weight at 220 lb (100 kg), and points heavier riders or those regularly tackling rough roads and hills toward the more expensive Apollo Go instead.

Despite the lower price, there’s still a surprisingly long feature list. The Dash gets IP66 water resistance, self-healing tubeless tires, regenerative braking, built-in turn signals, Apollo’s Beam stem light, NFC unlocking, Apple Find My integration, and connectivity with the Apollo smartphone app.

Apollo says it put the frame through 10,000 km (6,200 miles) of durability validation before shipping production units. A beta test program also accumulated 1,002 miles over 144 rides without any reported punctures or maintenance issues. Beta riders averaged 19 miles (30 km) per ride while finishing with 29% battery remaining, according to Apollo.

Perhaps the bigger story here, though, is where Apollo is positioning the Dash. The company says 52% of its customers previously owned scooters from brands including Segway, NIU, and Xiaomi, but historically moving up to an Apollo often meant crossing the $1,000 mark.

At $599, the Dash appears designed to get those riders into Apollo’s ecosystem much earlier – while also putting the company squarely into competition with the mass-market scooters it previously sat above.

And for those wanting a bit more power or features, Apollo’s more upmarket models are on sale now as part of early Black Friday promotions.

#4963. Binance Hack Wallets Move $137 Million in Ethereum After Two Weeks of Silence

Binance Hack Wallets Move $137 Million in Ethereum After Two Weeks of Silence

Wallets linked to the $6.3 billion Binance hack started moving large amounts of stolen Ethereum on Tuesday, breaking more than two weeks of inactivity as U.S. authorities continue trying to recover the funds.

Blockchain security service MistTrack reported that one address associated with the October 29 theft sent 34,279.66 ETH, worth roughly $63 million at the time, to three newly created wallets. The address had not moved its funds since October 30.

The on-chain record shows three outgoing transactions shortly before 7 a.m. UTC: two transfers of 12,000 ETH ($21.2 million) each and a third of about 10,279.66 ETH ($18.2 million). MistTrack said the receiving wallets had not sent the ether onward when it posted its initial alert.

That $63 million movement was only part of Tuesday’s activity. A second wallet linked to the same hack transferred another 40,600.66 ETH, worth roughly $74 million, across five transactions.

Analytics firm Arkham shows wallets linked to the attack had transferred a combined 74,880 ETH, valued at $136.8 million, over roughly three hours, and funds were beginning to be swapped for Bitcoin through Thorchain, a service that moves and exchanges crypto between blockchains.

The transfers come five days after the Justice Department said courts had authorized it to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion connected to the Binance theft. That sum is part of more than $2.7 billion frozen through combined government and private-sector action, not an additional recovery on top of it.

The FBI has attributed the attack to TraderTraitor, a hacking operation it links to North Korea’s military. The October 29 breach drained roughly $6.43 billion from Binance across 11 blockchains, according to blockchain analytics company Elliptic, making it the largest known crypto theft. North Korea denied involvement.

The attackers rushed to convert stablecoins into assets their issuers could not freeze. Tether and Circle can block stolen tokens on their networks, but ordinary ETH and Bitcoin do not have a central company capable of stopping a particular wallet from sending them.

Elliptic’s November 16 analysis put the frozen or seized portion at $2.85 billion and said the main Bitcoin and ETH holdings were still sitting in attack-linked addresses. That was before Tuesday’s transfers, which show at least part of the previously stationary ETH has begun moving.

The attackers had also spread their activity across almost 27,000 wallets and more than 83,000 transactions. That complicated trail does not mean they have managed to turn most of the stolen assets into cash.

Elliptic said only a negligible amount had likely been cashed out as of its latest assessment. To do that at scale, the holders would typically need an exchange or an over-the-counter broker willing to take the funds, creating another point at which authorities could identify and potentially block them.

#4961. What’s happening to crypto startup valuations?

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Sign up to get 2 days early access to each edition of The Funding

Last month, Axios reported, citing multiple sources, that Visa was in late-stage talks to acquire Fireblocks for about $2.5 billion. That's less than a third of the $8 billion valuation Fireblocks reached in January 2022. Fireblocks denied the report, telling The Block, "This is news to us, and not true at present time."

Regardless of the denial, the reported price brings up the question of how much private crypto companies are worth now, particularly those that raised capital during the 2021-22 boom.

Sources said the reported Fireblocks price is not far from what they have been seeing in secondary markets, whether or not Visa ends up buying the company.

"On our network, 2021-22 infrastructure supply has been quoted 60% to 70% under the last round for over a year," said Omar-Shakeeb Zahir, co-founder and CEO of SecondLane, a crypto secondary market platform. "A $2.5 billion figure against $8 billion sits right in that range."

A number of crypto companies haven't raised new funding since 2021 or 2022, so their last reported valuations are outdated. But shares sold by investors and employees in secondary markets are now going for much lower prices.

"A 2021 round is worth about 40 cents on the dollar today," Zahir said.

Jonas Thiele, founder and CEO of crypto secondary market platform OFFX, also said shares of well-known 2021-22 unicorns are regularly offered at discounts of 50% to 70%, "and for some of the biggest names of that cycle it's closer to 90%."

Karl-Martin Ahrend, co-founder and managing partner at crypto investment banking and M&A advisory firm Areta, estimated discounts of 50% to 80% across secondary markets and M&A. Thomas Klocanas, managing partner at Strobe Ventures, said secondary prices for late-stage companies from 2021 are generally 40% to 70% below their last rounds, "often with no clearing bid at all."

Data from secondary markets is helpful because many companies haven't had a new funding round to establish a lower valuation. Of the 49 companies in Architect Partners' database that raised at valuations of $1 billion or more in 2021 and 2022, 25 haven't announced another financing, said Adlee Heshmat, senior financial analyst at Architect Partners, a crypto investment bank and M&A advisor.

"Down rounds aren't really announced," Heshmat said, adding: "The repricing tends to show up in secondaries and in M&A, where buyers are pricing off of today's revenue, strategic fit and public company multiples."

Some crypto companies are still getting much higher valuations than in the past, especially in payments, stablecoins, trading and prediction markets. But many companies that raised at the 2021 peak are now trading at large discounts to their last funding rounds.

"It's a two-speed market. Most companies that raised at the 2021-22 peak trade well below those rounds today," Thiele said. "What holds up is anything with real revenue and a regulatory moat."

Heshmat made a similar point, saying, "It's a two-tier market right now. At the very top, valuations are now above the 2021-22 peak." Since last November, Architect Partners has tracked eight funding rounds at valuations of $20 billion or more, involving Ripple, Kraken, Kalshi, Polymarket, Crypto.com and OKX.

Only one company reached that level in 2021 and 2022, Heshmat said. He didn't name the company, but it was likely FTX, which raised at a $32 billion valuation in early 2022 before collapsing that November.

"Below that, late-stage valuations are still about a third under early 2022 averages," Heshmat added.

Payments and stablecoin companies have raised 39% more funding this year than at the same point in 2022, while funding for NFTs, gaming, protocols and developer tools is down around 80%, according to Heshmat.

The sources pointed to revenue as one of the main reasons some companies are doing better than others.

"There isn't one private crypto market anymore. There are two, and they barely talk to each other," Klocanas said. "Businesses with revenue, licenses and real distribution are at or above 2021 levels. The token-first infrastructure of the last cycle is the opposite," he added.

Klocanas said stablecoin payments, onchain credit, regulated trading and tokenization are doing better than other crypto sectors. General-purpose Layer 1 and Layer 2 blockchains, middleware companies with no revenue and consumer apps that depend on token incentives have struggled.

Prediction markets are one area where valuations have risen sharply, with Kalshi and Polymarket among the biggest players.

Ahrend said there are only a few major prediction market companies, and their valuations depend on whether they can stay ahead of competitors. On stablecoins, he said valuations "are justified for the few leaders with real volume, licenses and distribution, not for the long tail."

Klocanas agreed, saying: "The category leaders can grow into those numbers. The fifteenth stablecoin orchestration startup raising at 50x ARR [annual recurring revenue] probably can't."

The sources I spoke with said valuations were so high in 2021 largely because crypto markets were booming at the time.

"The 2021 rounds were priced in a hype phase," OFFX's Thiele said. "Crypto was a young market, trading volumes were at record highs, and investors were willing to pay for growth almost at any price."

Zahir said interest rates were near zero when those rounds took place. Valuation multiples were also high, and much of the revenue came from trading activity and token prices. Investors were expecting that growth to continue.

"Rates rose, multiples came back to earth, and much of that revenue never grew into the valuation," he said.

Lex Sokolin, co-founder and managing partner at Generative Ventures, said, "Multiple compression is at play — from 50x to 10x as growth stalls."

Thiele said many companies are still good businesses, "some much better than in 2021. But the price was set at the top of the cycle, and a company doesn't stay worth its peak valuation just because that was the last round."

Funds are also looking to get cash back from their investments, even if they have to sell shares for less than their last funding round prices. Founders also have to decide whether to wait for better prices or sell at current valuations.

"Funds now need DPI [distributions to paid-in capital], not TVPI [total value to paid-in capital]: cash at half the last round beats a paper 1x that never converts," Ahrend said.

Zahir also said, "LPs now judge on DPI, so GPs will use secondaries to sell parts of their winners and take the discount on the tail."

Some funds and early employees have already started selling their shares at discounts instead of waiting for the market to recover.

"For them, liquidity today is often worth more than a higher mark that may never be realized," Thiele said.

He added that founders must decide "whether to wait for the market to grow back into the old valuation or to accept a realistic price now."

Ahrend advised founders to start looking for buyers early. "Businesses are bought, not sold. Start a process with 18 months of runway, not six."

The sources I spoke to don't expect to see valuations across the crypto market returning to their 2021 highs. Though more exits that return cash to investors, clearer regulations, and revenue that can hold up when crypto prices fall could help some companies recover.

A few major IPOs and acquisitions could also help the market establish more realistic valuations.

"A few clean IPOs and strategic sales at sensible prices would reset the reference points for the whole market, more than any new funding round could," Thiele said.

Regulation is another factor. Stablecoins now have a U.S. law, but the crypto market structure bill stalled in the Senate in September, and with Democrats taking back control of the House and the Senate, the chances of further crypto legislation during President Donald Trump's second term are slim. Despite that, Klocanas said institutions need "predictability more than a single statute."

As for what could help valuations recover, Thiele said companies need revenue "that doesn't depend on token prices or trading volumes," while Sokolin said crypto businesses need to "challenge banks for their core money and lending offerings, or their capital markets venues."

Still, Ahrend expects many of the companies that raised during the 2021 boom to be acquired, recapitalized or shut down.

"Most of the 2021 class won't see peak marks again and will be resolved through M&A, recaps or wind-downs," Ahrend said. "2027 looks like a consolidation year."

Klocanas said returning to 2021 prices shouldn't be the goal. "We don't expect a broad recovery to 2021 levels, and in fact I don't think we should want one," he said. "What's ahead is a market that prices crypto companies like fintech and market infrastructure companies, on revenue, regulatory moat and distribution."

#4958. South Korea Set to Let 3,500 Companies Buy Bitcoin, Report Says

South Korea’s Financial Services Commission plans to publish guidelines next week that would let about 3,500 listed companies and professional-investor firms buy bitcoin and other crypto for investment, a local news agency reported Wednesday. The guidelines will be released alongside the government’s draft of the Digital Asset Basic Act, the country’s planned framework for the crypto industry, according to the report. The FSC plans to convene its Virtual Asset Committee to coincide with the release.

The FSC itself has not publicly announced a date for either release. If it lands next week, the move would come one year and nine months after the regulator laid out its roadmap for corporate crypto access in February 2025.

That plan opened the market in stages, with nonprofits and crypto service providers coming first, and only to sell. Since June 2025, Korean nonprofits have been able to cash out crypto they receive as donations, and World Vision made the first such trade, selling donated ether on Upbit.

Exchanges have also been allowed to sell their own holdings to cover operating costs. Coinone and DigitalX, formerly Korbit, have done so several times after filing disclosures. The second stage, which lets listed companies and firms registered as professional investors trade crypto for investment, was slated for the second half of 2025. It never arrived.

The holdup is market plumbing. Corporate orders are large, and even Korea’s biggest exchanges have limited room to absorb them without moving prices. “If companies come into the market and trade 5 billion won each, even an exchange like Upbit would struggle to take that volume,” an unnamed industry official told News1. Large trades would normally run through over-the-counter desks, and Korea does not yet have rules governing how those desks operate.

The Digital Asset Basic Act is meant to close that gap. It would split crypto service providers into separate business types and set entry and conduct rules for OTC desks, brokers and other operators, the report said. The government’s draft is expected to go to the National Assembly next week.

Lawmakers want it passed quickly. The Assembly’s Political Affairs Committee aims to process the bill by January, timed to the implementation of the U.S. GENIUS Act, the stablecoin law. Rep. Min Byung-duk of the Democratic Party said at a recent forum that he intends to “wrap it up by Jan. 9.”

The report did not describe what the corporate guidelines will contain. In January, *Seoul Economic Daily* reported that a draft would cap a company’s annual crypto investment at 5% of equity capital and limit purchases to the top 20 coins by market value. The FSC responded the next day that key items, including investment limits and eligible assets, had not been finalized.

Analysts said at the time that corporate flows would likely concentrate in bitcoin, the largest crypto asset by market value, even with a wider menu of coins. Jeonbuk Bank said in October it would offer bitcoin-backed loans. Korea has also pushed back its 22% crypto tax to 2029, the fourth delay, while the Bank of Korea has lobbied to write stablecoins into foreign exchange law.

Retail investors in Korea have been able to trade crypto for years. Domestic demand has at times pushed local bitcoin prices above global levels, a gap known as the kimchi premium. Corporate money has been the missing piece. Under the roadmap, about 3,500 companies would become eligible once the guidelines take effect.

The guidelines’ release would not on its own open trading. The FSC still has to say when listed companies can place their first orders.

#4956. Jensen Huang Says He'd Be 'Honored' to Return as Nvidia CEO, Despite Board's Resistance

Jensen Huang Says He'd Be 'Honored' to Return as Nvidia CEO, Despite Board's Resistance

Jensen Huang said Tuesday he would be willing to return as Nvidia CEO, publicly addressing the campaign to reinstate him less than a week after his abrupt departure. His offer came despite reports that the chipmaker's board has no plans to bring him back.

“If the Board of Directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honored to answer these calls and return as CEO,” Huang wrote in a statement on X.

The Nvidia co-founder also apologized for the conduct that triggered his exit. “I did not live up to the standards expected of everyone at NVIDIA, and I have made serious mistakes in my personal life,” he wrote. “I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret.”

“I own my mistakes completely, and I am committed to cooperating with the Board’s independent investigation,” he added.

Over the past week, I’ve been overwhelmed by the love and support of friends, industry colleagues, and people I've never met. Thank you to everyone who has shown kindness to my family and me. It means the world to me.

I came to the United States 53 years ago, and becoming an…

— Jensen Huang (@JensenHuang) November 17, 2026

His X profile underwent a separate change around the same time. By Tuesday, Huang had removed “CEO” from his bio, which now identifies him only as “Founder of NVIDIA”; the company banner and corporate affiliation badge also disappeared.

Huang resigned last Thursday after 33 years leading the chipmaker he founded in 1993. His departure followed disclosures about extramarital relationships involving Nvidia employees and a business executive whom U.S. officials suspect of connections to Chinese intelligence. Those intelligence allegations have not been established as fact.

The board's independent investigation is examining his conduct and whether sensitive company information or national security were put at risk. Nvidia has said it has no reason to believe company secrets were compromised; chief financial officer Colette Kress is serving as interim CEO.

“Since my departure, I’ve seen an outpouring of calls from NVIDIA’s employees, investors, business partners, and customers for me to return as CEO,” Huang wrote. He said many were reaching out directly as Nvidia navigates “complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics.” “NVIDIA is a once-in-a-generation company,” he added.

But the board's position—at least as of Tuesday—is that directors are focused on the investigation, not reinstatement.

The company is simultaneously looking for a successor. Directors reportedly approached Arm CEO Rene Haas, a former Nvidia executive, according to the Financial Times, although Nvidia said it had not formally engaged candidates and called speculation premature.

The movement to restore Huang has nevertheless attracted powerful allies. President Donald Trump said Monday he “would certainly not mind” a comeback, while Elon Musk backed a petition demanding Huang's reinstatement that had drawn nearly 192,000 signatures by Monday.

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman offered a more qualified defense. Altman, who was briefly fired and reinstated at OpenAI in 2023, said Huang had “long been a hero of mine” and that “heroes have flaws,” but stopped short of saying Nvidia should rehire him.

Huang also made a patriotic appeal, especially considering his outsting was especially rushed not because of his affairs but because of the possibility of him leaking secrets to China. “I came to the United States 53 years ago, and becoming an American citizen is one of the greatest gifts and proudest moments of my life,” he wrote.

“NVIDIA has been my life’s work,” he continued. “Nowhere but in America could a company like NVIDIA be possible. I love our country, and I love NVIDIA.”

He closed by thanking supporters and telling his family, “I love you, and I will spend the rest of my life being worthy of yours.” Nvidia, meanwhile, is continuing its permanent CEO search, with Kress scheduled to lead the company's quarterly earnings call later today.

#4955. Coinbase AI defense blocks $4.7 million from being sent to deepfake Elon Musk scam

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The company said the system prevented about 91% of the attempted transfers linked to the fake Tesla livestream, with no human intervention.

Coinbase Coinbase Global, Inc. (COIN) said its AI-powered scam defenses stopped $4.7 million in funds that customers tried to send to scammers who used an Elon Musk deepfake to promote a fake Tesla crypto giveaway in a livestream. The company said the system prevented about 91% of the attempted transfers, with no humans in the loop.

According to Coinbase's blog post published on Tuesday, the incident occurred during Tesla's "flying car" demonstration last month. Scammers created a YouTube livestream that resembled Tesla's official broadcast, featuring a deepfake of Elon Musk. It also featured on-screen wallet addresses and a QR code, offering to double any cryptocurrency sent. More than 500,000 people watched the stream, according to Coinbase.

Within roughly two hours, hundreds of Coinbase customers attempted to send millions of dollars to the addresses shown.

"The part that makes this hard: nobody was hacked," Coinbase said. "No Coinbase account was broken into, no password stolen, no credential phished. Hundreds of customers, many of them long-time users, decided on their own to send money to a stranger."

While most platforms have adopted real-time fraud detection systems, Coinbase explained that it is difficult for them to stop transactions that customers were actually tricked into authorizing.

Coinbase said its AI defense system closed that loophole by flagging the activity in real time using machine-learning models that score risky sends, including those matching giveaway patterns, brand impersonation, and newly created wallet addresses.

Then, it dispatched a dedicated scam-prevention agent to examine the context of the transaction, explain how the scams are structured, and ask questions that deconstruct the story scammers were pitching.

"What's notable is that the agent had never seen this scam. Deepfake giveaways tied to a live product launch weren't in its training," Coinbase said. "It recognized the shape of the scheme anyway, a promise to multiply funds, the urgency of an event that would end in hours, a transfer that could not be reversed, and it ran from the first flagged send to the last, with no humans in the loop."

Coinbase said it will continue to invest heavily in AI defense to create models that keep pace with new scam types through AI-driven research.

#4954. Amazon set to deploy more than 3,000 TATA electric delivery trucks

Amazon isn’t just electrifying its American delivery operations. The virtual big box stores that Bezos built understands the benefits of EVs as well as anyone, and they’re working hard to deploy electric vehicles everywhere they can. And now, that global push is extending to India.

Indian automaker TATA Motors has announced a deal with Amazon to deploy 3,000 electric trucks across Amazon’s transportation network in India. When it goes live, it will be the largest electric truck deployment by in India and mark Amazon’s most significant move to extend its Indian fleet electrification beyond last-mile logistics.

“India is accelerating towards electric mobility and the realization of Viksit Bharat 2047,” says Shri H.D. Kumaraswamy, Hon’ble Union Minister for Heavy Industries and Steel, Government of India. “The collaboration between TATA Motors and Amazon shows what that looks like in practice: sustained demand from a nationwide logistics network met by electric trucks manufactured in India. Through PM E-DRIVE, the Government will continue to support this transition, and I look forward to more such efforts in powering green logistics.”

In addition to being a big deal for TATA, the 3,000 electric truck deal represents a serous bump to India’s deployed electric truck fleet, and are a major step towards the wide-scale electrification of freight transportation across the country – that’s not just good for Amazon’s fuel bills, it’s good for everyone and everything that breathes air, too.

“Electrifying our logistics and transportation network is an important part of Amazon’s goal to achieve net-zero carbon emissions by 2040,” says Abhinav Singh, Vice President, Operations, Amazon, APAC, Middle East, Africa and Turkey. “We have made significant progress in India, with more than 15,000 electric vehicles operating across 500-plus cities. To enable this transition, we are also supporting the development of infrastructure and solutions that make EV adoption easier for our carrier partners and drivers. We are now setting a bold vision to scale electric trucking, addressing one of the toughest challenges in decarbonizing transportation. Our collaboration with TATA Motors marks an important step towards realizing this vision. By combining our strengths, we aim to accelerate the deployment of electric trucks across more routes, starting with intracity operations and expanding to India’s major freight corridors.”

The truck Amazon will be deploying in these record numbers is a version of the Tata Ultra E.7, a 7.5-tonne (~16,000 lb.) straight truck powered by a 240 kW (~320 hp) electric motor drawing power from a 96 kWh LFP battery pack. The truck’s makers claim a loaded range of up to 130 km (~80 miles) on a single charge, with DC fast-charging that’s capable enough to keep the trucks charged up between deliveries.

Amazon’s new TATA electric delivery trucks will support the transport of goods between warehouse facilities – which means traveling across cities and along India’s national freight corridors.

#4952. Bitcoin Price Trades Near $64,000 as Fed’s Waller Floats Half-Point Hike

Bitcoin price traded near $64,000 on Wednesday morning, according to Bitcoin Magazine data, as Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller said a half-point rate hike is probably needed in December. The bitcoin price had slipped below $64,000 on Tuesday afternoon. It climbed back to about $65,000 that evening after Nvidia beat earnings estimates, then faded through the U.S. morning.

The bitcoin price is down about 21% from a month ago, when it traded above $81,000.

Waller told CNBC in a live interview that the Fed probably needs a half-point increase at its Dec. 8-9 meeting. He said the central bank should reverse its three quarter-point cuts from last year and may need to do more. “You might need to, like, shock people,” Waller said.

“I’m very, very concerned about inflation. It’s kind of headed in the wrong direction, and it’s spreading out,” he said. Waller said the Fed should deliver the larger move if it is not prepared to signal a faster pace of hikes in the coming months. He also said about 70% of items in the core services basket are running above 3%, which has nothing to do with oil.

The 2-year Treasury yield rose more than 4 basis points to 4.83% after his comments. The dollar hit a 19-month high, and gold dropped 2.5% to its lowest level in more than a year. Futures markets fully price a quarter-point hike in December and another increase in January, CNBC reported. St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem suggested Tuesday that as many as four hikes could come by spring.

Minutes from the Fed’s Oct. 27-28 meeting are due Wednesday afternoon. Policymakers held rates at 3.75% to 4% in a 10-2 vote, with Fed Governor Lisa Cook and Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack dissenting in favor of a hike.

Nvidia reported third-quarter revenue of $115.84 billion after the bell Tuesday, more than double a year earlier and above the $109.1 billion analysts expected. Data center revenue more than doubled to $107.6 billion, and the company guided to about $130 billion for the current quarter. Interim CEO Colette Kress said Nvidia will remain “disciplined in how we commit capital.” The board said it would not reinstate Jensen Huang, who resigned as CEO last week.

Nvidia shares were up about 2.6% in premarket trading. Nasdaq-100 futures gained 0.8% and S&P 500 futures rose 0.3%, but the bitcoin price did not follow tech higher. IREN (IREN), the bitcoin miner turned AI cloud provider, rose between 8% and 10% in extended trading along with CoreWeave and Nebius, and was recently up about 3% premarket. Strategy (MSTR) closed down 6.3% at $96.50 on Tuesday and was little changed before the open, while Coinbase (COIN) closed down 4% and was up about 0.5% premarket.

U.S. spot bitcoin ETFs shed a net $239.9 million on Tuesday, a day after snapping a seven-session outflow streak. BlackRock’s IBIT accounted for $209.5 million of that. Anthropic is expected to price its IPO Wednesday evening, with shares set to start trading on the Nasdaq on Thursday under the ticker ANT. The company marketed 825 million shares at $70 to $78 each, and sources told CNBC on Tuesday that the deal will likely price above that range.

President Donald Trump arrived in Shenzhen, China, on Wednesday for a summit with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin. AI chip exports are a key issue.

A break of the weekend low near $62,000 would put last week’s flush back in view for the bitcoin price.

At the time of writing, the bitcoin price is near $63,800.

#4951. World Bank Group taps Circle and Citi to test stablecoin-based fund disbursements

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The initiative plans to initially focus on an intermediated and interoperable stablecoin-based payment model, with participation from borrower countries.

The World Bank Group (WBG) said it is exploring the use of regulated USD stablecoins with Citi and Circle to speed up and cut the cost of fund disbursements.

In a statement released Tuesday, the international organization said that it plans to test blockchain-based payment rails alongside traditional correspondent banking to improve existing WBG funds disbursement arrangements.

"Central to this initiative will be Citi's existing connections to established banking rails and Circle's stablecoin payments infrastructure to mint and settle funds in USDC," the group said.

The initiative is set to build on WBG's existing FundsChain platform, which tracks flows across more than 350 projects in 96 countries. The project plans to initially focus on an intermediated and interoperable stablecoin-based payment model, with participation from borrower countries.

"This initiative is about exploring ways to make the financial plumbing of development work better for the people who need it most," WBG President Ajay Banga said in the statement. "Enabling money to move faster and at lower cost means real development impact – a health clinic gets built on time, a farmer gets paid before the harvest season ends, and a government can be more responsive to pressing needs."

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said it is building an internet-native financial system, and "development finance is one of its most important applications."

"Money that moves at the speed of the internet shouldn't be reserved for the world's biggest banks and corporations; it should reach the people and places that need it most, with full visibility into where every dollar goes," Allaire added.

The World Bank Group is a multilateral development institution owned by 189 member countries. It comprises five organizations that work to reduce poverty and support sustainable growth in developing economies.

In the fiscal year ended June 30, 2026, the group delivered $123.5 billion in loans, grants, equity investments, and guarantees to partner countries and private businesses, according to its latest annual report. This includes $35.8 billion in Sub-Saharan Africa, $25.1 billion in Latin America and the Caribbean, and $24.6 billion in Europe and Central Asia.

#4949. Ethereum Devs Weigh a Scaling Upgrade That Could Break 15,000 Apps

Ethereum Devs Weigh a Scaling Upgrade That Could Break 15,000 Apps

Ethereum’s upcoming Glamsterdam upgrade threatens to break as many as 15,000 smart contracts, developers warned during a core developer call Monday, forcing a debate over how much disruption the network should tolerate in the name of scaling.

The concern centers on two proposed changes, EIP-8037 and EIP-8038, designed to let Ethereum scale from 60 million gas to as much as 200 million gas per block, a roughly 3.3x jump that developers say would sharply lower costs for most users while keeping state growth in check.

The problem, flagged late in the development process, is that the changes break certain invariants, assumptions that some smart contracts were built to rely on.

“Multiple contracts were relying on invariants in the protocol that are modified by 8037 and 8038,” said Marius van der Wijden, an Ethereum Foundation developer who worked on the scaling effort.

He acknowledged that the team “wanted to do significant public outreach” but that timelines slipped amid reorganizations and budget cuts at the foundation. He said he shares “a lot of blame” as the last remaining scaling lead still at the EF.

Two security firms, ChainSecurity and Dedaub, presented findings from AI-assisted audits, with the combined search turning up roughly 200 distinct classes of affected contracts, most tied to gas-cost increases rather than the new mechanics themselves.

Van der Wijden put the highest estimate at 10,000 to 15,000 contracts, a “fringe subset” of Ethereum’s roughly 90 million, with impacted value he loosely pegged in the low hundreds of thousands of dollars among contracts not yet committed to upgrading.

App developers pushed back on the framing. Jango, who builds the Juicebox protocol, warned the change undermines “composability,” a core Ethereum value prop, and shakes “developer confidence in building immutable protocols.” A Uniswap developer urged that EIP-8037 “should definitely be reconsidered.”

The Ethereum Foundation already delayed Glamsterdam’s testnet fork over these gas-related security flaws. Developers stressed Monday that no decision has been made, with the question headed to Thursday’s execution-layer call.

But several signaled a strong desire to ship the fork as planned, arguing that removing the changes could delay Glamsterdam, and the broader roadmap toward post-quantum security, by four to six months, time they say Ethereum can’t spare as quantum and AI threats loom.

#4946. New York State Pension Fund Boosts Strategy (MSTR) Stake by More Than 10%

The New York State Common Retirement Fund raised its stake in Strategy (MSTR) by more than 10% in the third quarter, to 352,850 shares worth $54.0 million at Sept. 30. The pension fund disclosed the position in a Form 13F filed Tuesday, covering the quarter ended Sept. 30. It added 33,000 shares from the 319,850 it reported at June 30.

That is an increase of a little more than 10%. The filing does not say why the pension fund added the shares. The reported value nearly doubled, to $54.0 million from $27.8 million. Strategy’s stock closed the quarter at $153.09, up from $86.93 at midyear, so most of the gain in dollar value came from the rally, not from the added shares.

The fund is one of the largest public pension plans in the U.S. It holds the assets used to pay retirement benefits for New York’s state and local public employees. Comptroller Thomas P. DiNapoli, the fund’s trustee, valued it at an estimated $309.7 billion as of June 30, the latest official figure. There is not yet a published valuation for Sept. 30.

Public equities are only part of the portfolio. As of March 31, they accounted for 39.4% of the fund’s assets, with the rest in cash and bonds, private equity, real estate and other alternatives. The 13F lists 2,975 holdings worth $71.3 billion. The Strategy stake is a small slice of that book, less than a tenth of a percent.

Strategy has made bitcoin its primary treasury asset. The company held 850,849 bitcoin as of Nov. 15, with an aggregate purchase price of $64.20 billion, or $75,452 a coin. Institutional managers have 45 days after quarter-end to file a 13F. Tuesday’s report is the first official look at the pension fund’s third-quarter buying, after the stock had already fallen sharply from the $153.09 price used in the filing.

Strategy closed Tuesday at $96.50, below $100 and about 37% under its Sept. 30 close. At that price, the same 352,850 shares are worth about $34 million, roughly $20 million less than the value in the filing. The common shares also finished under Strategy’s STRC preferred stock, which closed at $98.70. That is the first time they have closed below the preferred since mid-August.

The stock is down about 20% from its Nov. 10 close of $121.07. A 13F is a snapshot. It does not show whether the pension fund bought or sold shares in October or November, when most of that decline took place.

The information table lists no shares of BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust or other spot bitcoin ETFs, and no shares of Strategy’s preferred stock. The pension fund does hold Coinbase (COIN). It reported 109,433 shares at Sept. 30, a little below the 111,633 shares it held at midyear. The reported value still rose, to $20.4 million from $16.3 million, as Coinbase’s stock climbed.

It also holds a string of bitcoin miners. IREN, listed in the filing as Iris Energy, was the largest of those positions, at 154,600 shares worth $6.3 million, up from 140,100 shares at midyear. The fund trimmed most of its other mining stakes during the quarter. The remaining names included Hut 8, TeraWulf, Riot, Cipher, Core Scientific, MARA Holdings, CleanSpark, Bitdeer and Bit Digital.

Together, the mining shares were worth about $21.6 million at Sept. 30. Even added together, the Strategy, Coinbase and mining positions were worth about $96 million, a thin sliver of the 13F book. California’s public employees’ pension fund, CalPERS, held a slightly larger Strategy position at the same date: 374,740 shares worth $57.4 million, its 13F filed Monday shows.

The next 13Fs, covering the fourth quarter, are due in February.

#4944. Hong Kong central bank explores agentic AI to detect crypto-related financial crime

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

HKMA Executive Director Raymond Chan said AI could help analyze both onchain transfers and offchain customer transactions to flag anomalies.

The Hong Kong Monetary Authority is exploring the use of agentic AI to monitor crypto asset transactions and detect money-laundering risks, Executive Director Raymond Chan said.

During a Tuesday speech at a workshop hosted by the HKMA, Chan said that AI could help analyze both onchain transfers and offchain customer transactions to flag anomalies like rapid fund layering through switches between crypto and fiat.

The HKMA, Hong Kong's de facto central bank, aims to accelerate the adoption of AI tools to make financial crime monitoring more effective and efficient, Chan said.

"Imagine how effective AML controls would become if we could integrate blockchain analytics, customer due diligence information, scam intelligence, monitoring of suspicious wallets and rapid freezing into an on-chain AI agent," he said.

Chan noted that a key challenge lies in quickly linking wallet activities to real-world identities to prevent illicit fund dissipation.

"We are acutely aware that no single tool offers complete protection," said Chan. "Our defenses must rely on multi-layered safeguards that integrate advanced technologies."

The HKMA official warned that criminal networks are increasingly exploiting digital assets for illicit activities, including fraud and human trafficking. "This occurs because digital assets present unique risks by combining speed, global reach, pseudonymity and limited reversibility," he added.

Notably, illicit addresses received more than $150 billion in 2025, with stablecoins accounting for 84% of those transactions, according to Chan, citing data from blockchain analysis firm Chainalysis.

Chan also highlighted the Virtual Asset Intelligence Taskforce (VAIT), which was established in November 2025 to strengthen Hong Kong's efforts to combat crypto-related crime.

The public-private task force brings together the Hong Kong police, the HKMA, the Customs and Excise Department, the Securities and Futures Commission, and licensed crypto trading platforms. The initiative has improved intelligence quality and strengthened both prevention and detection, according to Chan.

"Crucially, VAIT treats crypto crime as an ecosystem issue – spanning platforms, regulators, and law enforcement – rather than addressing it as a series of isolated incidents," he said.
